Q.WHAT ADDITIONAL SUM DOES THE COMPANY REQUEST FOR ITS UTILITY SCALE AND DG RFPS?
A.Consistent with the additional sum approved in the 2022 IRP, the Company requests a levelized $4.00 per kilowatt-year (“kW-yr”) AC of the procured amount annually for the term of each PPA entered into as a result of its Utility Scale and DG RFPs. Such an additional sum appropriately incents the Company to competitively procure additional resources and fairly considers lost revenues, changed risks, and an equitable sharing of benefits consistent with the additional sum authorized by statute. 
Q.HOW DOES THE COMPANY PROPOSE TO RECOVER THE COSTS RELATED TO ITS RENEWABLE PROCUREMENTS?
A.Consistent with past practice, the Company proposes to recover the costs related to the procurement of renewable energy through the fuel cost recovery (“FCR”) clause. Any participation charges designed to recover associated costs and paid by subscribing customer(s) (as described below) would be applied to the fuel clause to reduce FCR costs. 
iv.Renewable and resiliency customer programs
Customer Renewable Subscription Programs
Q.WHY IS THE COMPANY PROPOSING TO MAKE ALL THE ENERGY PROCURED THROUGH THE UTILITY SCALE AND DG RFPS AVAILABLE FOR SUBSCRIPTION?
A.Many Georgia Power customers continue to express increased interest in renewable energy programs. Consistent with the approach taken in the 2022 IRP and in response to growing customer demand, the Company proposes to continue to make the energy from every MW procured by the Utility Scale and DG RFPs available for subscription by qualifying customers. Through the Company’s competitive procurement and subscription processes, renewable energy is procured to the benefit of all customers, while also allowing interested individual customers to access the attributes of these resources directly.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE proposed modifications to the CARES PROGRAM.
A.The Company proposes enhancements to the CARES program to increase flexibility, expand customer participation, and align renewable procurement with demand while mitigating financial risks to non-participating customers. The Utility Scale RFP process has been modified to allow for additional procurement opportunities if customer subscription demand is not met through the traditional RFP process, ensuring that interested customers have the opportunity to drive additional renewable procurement beyond initial targets. To improve the subscription process, the Company proposes to refine the NOI process, enabling greater customer participation through a Commission-approved methodology. Subscription terms now range from 10 to 30 years in five-year increments, with customers offered two pricing options: a renewable energy credit (“REC”)-based fixed program portfolio charge without an hourly energy credit, or a fixed program charge based on the PPA price with an associated hourly energy credit.
To reduce financial risks to non-participating customers, the pricing methodology for the hourly energy credit will be modified, introducing reimbursement thresholds to ensure fairness and prevent negative impacts to nonparticipants. Increased subscription flexibility has also been incorporated to allow for increased procurement through the Phase I submission refresh process to buy down project costs if customer demand exceeds the initial megawatts procured. This enables additional projects from the competitive tier or target list, as appropriate, to revise their submission prices in a way that maintains or improves overall program benefits. If further subscription needs remain unmet, the RFP process will be extended through the proposed Phase II process to allow additional projects to be submitted for consideration. Through this process, customers would have the option to propose specific renewable resources for procurement through a CIR option, negotiating directly with developers, as well as the ability to subscribe to incremental resources at higher subscription prices that offset higher PPA costs. Successful proposals in this process will result in a PPA between Georgia Power and the facility owner, and a corresponding CARES Customer Agreement between Georgia Power and the subscribing customer. All RECs from these CIRs will be retired on behalf of the subscribing customer.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE CARES DG SUBSCRIPTION program.
A.The Company proposes to expand the CARES program by offering subscriptions to resources procured through Georgia Power’s DG RFPs. This expansion will provide more Georgia Power customers with the opportunity to participate in the CARES Program, as this part of the program will be available to eligible C&I customers with an aggregate demand between 1 MW and 3 MW, as well as Residential customers. Initial subscriptions will be available to eligible C&I customers from the initial procurement phase of each DG RFP. These customers will participate in the CARES NOI process similar to the Utility Scale CARES program. If C&I customer demand for subscriptions exceeds the MW procured through the DG RFP, the Company will initiate the Phase I submission refresh process (described previously) and Phase II of the DG RFP, if needed. 
The DG Community Solar Program will allocate up to 10 MW of the initial 50 MW target from each DG RFP for subscription by residential customers. This Program will provide customers with access to solar from new DG facilities using a simplified CARES subscription methodology. Under this approach, residential customers will subscribe to solar energy through a pricing mechanism based on the PPA price, with energy credits calculated from the average value of the facility’s production, factoring in Georgia Power’s hourly operating costs of incremental generation per kWh. RECs will be retired on behalf of participating customers based on the actual output of the facilities.
Georgia Power is exploring opportunities to partner with third parties to reduce subscription prices for lower-income customers, thereby enhancing the value proposition for eligible participants. If demand exceeds the available MW allocation for residential customers, or if additional demand for DG subscriptions is identified from other customers, additional resources may be procured through the extended RFP process, which will allow developers the opportunity to bring in new subscribers and drive the need for incremental projects.
Customer-Sited Renewable Programs
Q.DOES THE COMPANY PROPOSE ANY CUSTOMER-SITED RENEWABLE PROGRAMS?
A.Yes. The Company seeks Commission approval of the Customer-Sited Solar Plus Storage Pilot, which will enhance options for customers interested in installing renewable resources at their premises. Additionally, the Company seeks to modify the existing Customer Connected Solar Program to add storage resources, increase the size of eligible facilities, and allow customers without billing history to participate. These programs expand upon Georgia Power’s existing portfolio of customer-sited options to now include storage resources, enhancing customer resiliency and grid value through dispatchable capacity-benefitting resources.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE Customer-Sited Solar Plus Storage Pilot PROGRAM.
A.The Customer-Sited Solar Plus Storage Pilot Program is designed to encourage the installation of solar plus battery storage at residential and small commercial sites. The pilot targets 50 MW of capacity, divided equally into two participation pathways: Customer-Directed and Company-Directed. Participants in either pathway can enroll systems up to 20 kW (residential) or 250 kW (small commercial). The systems will be interconnected behind the meter (“BTM”), can be owned by the participating customer or a third party, and will be dispatched by the Company to deliver capacity value to the System. The pilot aims to improve grid reliability, evaluate customer preferences, and enhance economic viability of dispatchable customer-sited solar-plus-storage resources.
Q.Describe EACH PARTICIPATION PATHWAY FOR THE CUSTOMER-SITED SOLAR PLUS STORAGE PILOT.
A.Under the Customer-Directed model, customers direct the use of the battery and can respond to discrete utility-called events to receive incentive payments. In addition to an annual enrollment incentive of $15/kW, participating customers will receive payments based on the asset performance during called events of $1.50/kWh. New or existing assets are eligible to participate in this program. 
Under the Company-Directed model, Georgia Power will monitor and control the BESS and operate it based on System needs. In exchange, customers receive a one-time upfront incentive of $750/kW for a 10-year commitment. Only new assets will be eligible to participate, and systems must pair solar and storage. In both models, the BESS is available for resiliency use by the customer in times of grid outage.
Q.Are there any RATE LIMITations on which customers can participate? 
A.Yes. Although participating customers will retain their rate options, there are two proposed rate limitations: (1) participating residential customers cannot be on the residential (“R”) rate, and (2) participating commercial customers cannot be on the general service (“GS”) rate. These energy-only rates do not provide an appropriate time-of-use (“TOU”) or demand signal to incentivize operation of the solar and battery resources to maximize System benefit. Participating customers will be eligible for other riders, including the renewable and nonrenewable resources (“RNR”) rate. 
Q.Is there a PARTICIPATION cap on the pilot?
A.The Company has not proposed a cap on participation, but rather presented a target enrollment it believes is feasible to implement over the IRP cycle. In the event the Company reaches the target enrollment before the 2028 IRP, the Company will present to the Commission potential program modifications based on lessons learned and seek approval to continue adding interested customers. This “check-in” approach would allow for feedback and learnings to be incorporated without signaling an anticipated end to, or ceiling on, enrollment. 
Q.Did the Company collaborate with stakeholders in developing the solar plus storage pilot?
A.Yes. The Company participated in two collaboration sessions at the Commission and received additional feedback from participating stakeholders following those meetings. In addition, the Company had conversations with original equipment manufacturers (“OEMs”), installers, program facilitators, sources of potential complimentary funding, and customers. The Company also reviewed peer programs and talked with other utilities in the development process.
Q.DOES THE PILOT PROGRAM DESIGN ADDRESS FEEDBACK THE COMPANY RECEIVED THROUGH THE COLLABORATION PROCESS?
A. Yes. As described more fully in the IRP Main Document, the pilot program features a hybrid of upfront and ongoing compensation with incentives that are clear and simple to understand. As proposed, the pilot program allows for broad customer participation including those enrolled in time-varying rates, the RNR tariff, and other utility programs, and includes special consideration for low to moderate income customers. The pilot will leverage the battery inverter for measuring performance and does not penalize customers for non-performance. The program will support participation from multiple battery manufacturers; standalone storage; and existing systems. The pilot ensures at least 20% of battery capacity remains available for local resiliency, and provides for optionality to meet individual needs. By incorporating program design elements in consideration of direct feedback from stakeholders, that Company seeks to deliver a program that meets System, market, and customer needs.
Q.How did the company determine the incentive values to be offered for program participation?
A.The incentive values were calculated based on the forecasted avoided generation capacity value associated with assets participating in each pathway. The net present value over the term was discounted to 75%, applying a shared savings model consistent with other DER programs. The Company intends this design to ensure participating customers receive sufficient incentive to install dispatchable resources and participate in the program, while also ensuring non-participating customers receive value and do not subsidize implementation and program administration costs. 
In recognition of the unique needs of low-to-moderate income residential and municipal, university, school and hospital (“MUSH”) commercial customers, the discount factor was not applied to derive incentives for customers in these segments. Further, the program was designed to allow and enable stacking of additional funding sources including resources specifically targeting these groups of customers. 
Q.WHAT DOES THE COMPANY HOPE TO LEARN OR ACHIEVE THROUGH THIS PILOT PROGRAM?
A.The Company has designed the Customer-Sited Solar Plus Storage Pilot Program to provide the Company with valuable information regarding the technical capabilities, value, and market acceptance of customer-sited solar plus storage. 
Additionally, as described in the Main panel, the Company has requested enhanced control through its Distributed Energy Resource Management System (“DERMS”). The parallel participation pathways allow for validation of the benefits accompanying enhanced control. The Company’s DERMS will have the capability to dispatch DERs within program parameters, such that the Company will be able to optimize the operation to realize anticipated capacity value and explore additional use cases for solar plus storage and other programs.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE proposed MODIFICATIONS to the Customer Connected Solar Program.
A.Georgia Power proposes modifying the Customer Connected Solar Program by (1) increasing the facility site criteria to a 250 kW minimum and 6 MW maximum, (2) expanding the resource types to include BESS co-located with solar, and (3) allowing new customers to participate. The proposed enhancements are driven by stakeholder feedback. By expanding the eligibility criteria, the Company aims to fill the remaining 23+ MW to meet customer resiliency needs through this expanded front of the meter option. The capacity value created by the dispatchable storage systems will benefit all Georgia Power customers through enhanced reliability and affordability, will support customer resiliency goals, and support the growth of a sustainable customer-sited DG market in Georgia.
Other Customer Renewable Program Options 
Q.ARE THE CARES SUBSCRIPTION AND CUSTOMER-SITED PROGRAMS THE ONLY OPTIONS AVAILABLE FOR CUSTOMERS TO SUPPORT RENEWABLE RESOURCES?
A.No. As stated in the 2025 IRP Main Document, customers will continue to have options to offset their own energy consumption using BTM customer-sited resources, offset their own energy consumption and sell any excess output through the RNR tariff, participate in Community Solar or one of the Company’s REC purchase programs, or sell renewable energy to Georgia Power as a Qualifying Facility (“QF”) under the Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act of 1978 (“PURPA”). Georgia Power has interconnected more than 12,000 solar projects to date, including customer generators who choose to offset energy usage with BTM solar installations, through RNR or as a QF. 
Q.IS THE COMPANY PROPOSING TO MAKE ANY CHANGES TO THE COMMUNITY SOLAR PROGRAM?
A.No, Georgia Power is not requesting any modifications to the existing Community Solar Program. However, as described above, the Company has proposed the new DG Community Solar Program as an additional subscription option for residential customers.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE GEorgia power’s EXISTING Customer-SITED RENEWABLE Programs.
A.The Company interconnects and receives energy from customer generators through several Commission approved options. The Energy Offset option exists for customers who wish to install on-site generation without compensation for energy pushed back to the grid. The RNR program enrolls customer generators who offset some of their usage with renewable energy generated onsite and compensates these generators for energy pushed back to the grid at the Company’s Renewable Cost Benefit adjusted Solar Avoided cost, plus a $0.04 adder pursuant to the 2022 Rate Case. This adder creates additional costs for all customers as it pays customer generators more than the Company’s avoided cost for this energy. Approximately 5,000 customer generators are compensated at retail rates for all the energy produced from customer sited generation through the fully subscribed Monthly Netting program. This program also creates significant upward rate pressure for non-participating customers by compensating above the avoided cost value. Finally, the Company complies with its purchase obligations pursuant to PURPA through standard offer purchases from QFs. The pricing for these transactions is based on the Company’s projected day ahead hourly avoided costs and holds other customers harmless.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE EXISTING REC programs.
A.The Simple Solar Program is available to residential, commercial, and industrial customers, allowing them to match either 50% or 100% of their monthly energy usage with solar RECs retired on their behalf. The program operates on a monthly basis with no long-term commitment required. Since its launch in 2022, it has consistently served around 1,800 customers, resulting in approximately 27,000 RECs being retired annually.
The Flex REC Program originated in the 2022 IRP as a replacement for the Simple Solar Large Volume program. It enables Georgia Power to procure larger quantities of RECs to meet increasing customer demand. This program sources RECs from a diverse range of renewable resources, including solar and wind, and potentially other renewable sources. Since its inception, Flex REC has maintained an average of 16 customers per month, retiring about 300,000 RECs annually on behalf of participating customers.
The Retail REC Retirement (“R3”) program is designed for C&I customers who wish to claim renewable benefits from certain existing renewable resources. It allows these customers to subscribe to RECs from System resources that are either already operational or under construction. The RECs and associated environmental attributes, which would typically be retired on behalf of all customers, are instead retired specifically on behalf of the participating customer. Currently, there are no participants in this program, but it is expected to become a viable option as more customers aim to meet their carbon reduction goals within the next five years.
The Company is not proposing any modifications to these programs at this time.
Existing Customer Resiliency Programs
Q.please describe the der customer PILOT program that the Commission approved in the 2022 IRP.
A.Following the 2022 IRP, the Company developed the DER Customer Pilot Program alongside Commission Staff and intervenors, resulting in the Resiliency Asset Service (“RAS-1”) and Demand Response Credit (“DRC-1”) tariffs. These tariffs, approved in January 2023, provide a framework for customers seeking resiliency-focused solutions. For customers participating on RAS-1, Georgia Power will install and operate a dispatchable DER behind the customer’s meter. Participating customers pay a monthly service charge for that resiliency benefit. Customers participating in RAST-1 also have the option to participate in DRC-1, in which Georgia Power will provide a credit in exchange for the Company’s ability to use the DER for demand response during System reliability events. 
Q.please describe the der Colocation program that the Commission approved in the 2023 IRP Update.
A.The DER Colocation program, as approved in the 2023 IRP Update Order, is an optional tariff available to qualifying C&I customers. Under this program, Georgia Power owns, operates, maintains, and controls dispatchable DERs located on customer premises, which are then economically dispatched to provide energy and capacity benefits to all customers. The DER systems are interconnected to the electric grid, allowing for energy transmission while also serving as a resiliency resource for participating customers during outages. Participating customers pay rates that ensure the DER investment remains below its system value, thereby providing financial benefits to all customers. The DER technology used in this program may include combustion turbines, reciprocating internal combustion engines (“RICE”), and other dispatchable technology with a firm fuel supply.
Q.please describe the der customer-owned program that the Commission approved in the 2023 IRP Update.
A. The DER Customer-Owned Program is an optional program designed for qualifying C&I customers who own dispatchable DERs less than 10 MW. Under the DER Customer-Owned tariff (“DCO-1”), participating customers receive bill credits based on the capacity and energy value of their DERs. The program ensures that DER interconnections support the electric grid while also providing backup power during outages. Similar to the DER Colocation Program, a key difference under the DER Customer-Owned Program is that the customer retains ownership of the DER asset, while Georgia Power operates and controls the resource for economic dispatch.
Q.WHAT ARE THE COMPANY’S OBJECTIVES IN OFFERING THESE DER PROGRAMS TO CUSTOMERS?
A.DER programs are an important component of the Company’s portfolio of customer programs. The Company is in regular communication with customers and continues to modify its suite of DER options to better align with customer interest and Company needs. The design of the Company’s DER programs is intended to maximize mutual benefits among customers looking to make these types of onsite investments while leveraging the resilience and reliability benefits to the grid and all retail customers. In addition, the Company aims to encourage customers considering adding these resources to adopt cleaner resources. 
q.are any customers enrolled on these der tariffs?
A.Not yet, but the Company is working closely with customers to move forward with projects on its DER tariffs. Discussions are ongoing and several potential customers have progressed to evaluating contract language and site design elements. 
Q.IS THE COMPANY PROPOSING ANY MODIFICATIONS TO EXISTING DER PROGRAMS? 
A.Georgia Power is seeking to modify the DER Customer-Owned Program in the 2025 IRP to allow contract terms up to 15 years. Previous tariff language restricted contracts through 2031. Extending the potential contract duration increases the value proposition for both participating and non-participating customers. Participating customers will have increased certainty on the value stream to justify additional costs associated with participation. Non-participating customers receive the benefits of capacity locked in and procured at a discounted rate for an extended duration. 
Proposed Customer Resiliency Program 
Q.please describe the Large Customer Owned Resiliency program that georgia power is proPOsing in the 2025 irp.
A.The LCOR Program is a newly proposed program aimed at transmission-connected C&I customers. This program allows these customers to retain ownership of their DER assets while providing Georgia Power with operational certainty that contracted response will materialize when called upon by the Company for utility use. These assets are not intended to push back onto the grid and the resources do not need to be separately metered. Participating customers will be responsible for all fuel and O&M costs associated with the assets. As with the Company’s other DER programs, assets must be dispatchable with firm fuel supply and permitted for non-emergency use. By expanding its DER offerings, Georgia Power aims to provide a robust portfolio of options for customers with diverse energy needs while ensuring grid resilience and affordability.
Q.what are the benefits of the LCOR program?
A.Benefits of the LCOR Program include:
Economic Compensation: Participating customers are compensated for providing firm load reductions to the System and Georgia Power’s need for additional capital expenditures is reduced.
Faster Capacity Recognition: Unlike the supply-side DER programs that are subject to FERC-dictated interconnection timelines and requirements, this program accelerates the timeframe in which co-located resources can be utilized to meet capacity needs.
Reliability Enhancement: The program ensures that load reductions materialize when needed, reducing uncertainty, increasing efficiency, and improving grid stability.
Cost Savings for Non-Participants: Since the program operates under a shared savings model, the cost of procuring capacity is lower than traditional generation expansion, reducing costs for all customers.
Q.which customers are eligible to participate in the LCOR program?
A.The LCOR Program is designed for large C&I customers who meet the following eligibility criteria:
Customers must own and operate dispatchable DERs with a firm fuel supply permitted for non-emergency use.
The DERs must be connected to Georgia Power’s grid and be available to respond based on System needs during a contracted number of hours.
Customers must enter into a contractual agreement with Georgia Power to provide demand response contributions.
Participants must meet the minimum capacity commitment during events as defined in the customer contract.
This program is particularly suited for businesses with critical energy needs, such as data centers, manufacturing facilities, hospitals, and logistics hubs, where backup power and load flexibility can be leveraged for grid resiliency.
Electric Transportation
Q.WHAT IS VEHICLE-TO-EVERYTHING TECHNOLOGY?
A.Vehicle-to-everything (“V2X”) technology refers to the use of bi-directional charging technology that enables electric vehicles (“EVs”) to transfer energy stored in their batteries back to buildings, houses, or the grid. With increasing EV deployment, a significant number of batteries will be in the market, and unused energy in those batteries could be beneficial as a grid asset. 
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE COMPANY’S PROPOSAL FOR A VEHICLE TO GRID PILOT PROGRAM.
A.The Company seeks to install up to 10 bi-directional chargers for a grid-specific, or V2G, pilot program across up to four customer locations. The pilot will be available to public school systems served by Georgia Power that utilize or plan to utilize electric school buses as part of their fleet. 
Q.WHY IS A V2G SCHOOL BUS PILOT AN APPROPRIATE STARTING POINT TO EXPLORE V2X TECHNOLOGY?
A.School systems are particularly well-suited for V2G pilot programs for several reasons. First, school buses have regular, predictable schedules. They are typically in use during the morning and afternoon, idle for long periods during the day and overnight, and may not be in use at all during summer peak periods. This predictability makes it easier to manage charging and discharging cycles and utilize electric buses as a grid resource without impacting the school customer’s transportation availability. 
Second, school buses have large batteries that can store significant amounts of energy. This makes them ideal for providing substantial power back to the grid during peak demand times, and the scale of available capacity justifies interconnection and equipment costs. 
Third, school systems have already purchased electric buses and expressed interest in participating in a Georgia Power V2G program. Stakeholders submitted a V2G bus pilot idea as part of the DSMWG, and other utilities have launched pilots that can serve as a guide for implementation. Finally implementing V2G technology in school systems can serve as a model for other community-based projects, promoting broader adoption and awareness of V2G capabilities. 
Q.WHAT DOES THE COMPANY HOPE TO LEARN FROM THE V2G PILOT?
A.The Company hopes to gain a better understanding of the infrastructure costs required to support V2G and the ongoing costs to manage and administer such technology. The Company also seeks to learn about the capabilities of V2G and the value of vehicles as a grid resource to provide capacity during peak periods and other use cases. Finally, the Company seeks to understand how customer behavior and preferences will impact the potential for vehicles to serve as a reliable grid resource while being used primarily as a transportation solution. 

V.CONCLUSION
Q.WHAT ARE GEORGIA POWER’S REQUESTS FOR RENEWABLE RESOURCE PROCUREMENTS IN THIS CASE?
A.Georgia Power seeks Commission approval of the following renewable resource procurement requests: 
The updated Utility Scale RFP process to procure energy from 1,000 MW of new Utility Scale renewable energy resources, along with the ability to procure additional resources above the initial MW target to meet the needs of subscribing customers.
The updated DG RFP process to procure energy from 100 MW of new DG solar resources through two separate RFPs (50 MW each), including the incorporation of locational value in DG procurement evaluations and the ability to procure additional resources above the initial MW targets to meet the needs of subscribing customers.
The levelized additional sum of $4.00 / kW-yr AC of the total capacity amount from which renewable energy is procured from the Utility Scale and DG RFPs proposed in this IRP, annually for the term of each PPA.
Q.WHAT ARE GEORGIA POWER’S REQUESTS AS IT RELATES TO CUSTOMER PROGRAMS IN THIS CASE? 
A.Georgia Power seeks Commission approval of the following requests regarding its DSM Action Plan for the Proposed Case, DER Resiliency Programs, and customer renewable programs:
DSM:
Grant a certificate for the Residential Products program.
Decertify the Residential Refrigerator Recycling Plus program, the Residential Specialty Lighting program, and the Commercial Behavioral program.
Amend the certificate for four (4) previously certified programs: 
the Residential Behavioral program; 
the Residential Demand Response program; 
the Commercial Prescriptive Program; and 
the Small Commercial Direct Install program.
Grant a waiver of the TRC requirement within Commission Rule 51534.04(4)(a)(3) for four (4) previously certified programs: 
the Residential HopeWorks program; 
the Residential Home Energy Improvement program; 
the Residential Energy Assistance for Savings and Efficiency program; and 
the Commercial Custom program.
Approve the updated program economics for all previously certified DSM programs.
Approve the revised additional sum calculation methodology collected through the DSM programs certified in the 2025 DSM Application. 
Approve the Company’s other DSM activities as further specified in the Company’s 2025 IRP in Docket No. 56002, including the Energy Efficiency Awareness Initiative, pilot studies, and Learning Power Education Initiative.
DER Resiliency Programs:
Approve the modification of the DER Customer Owned Program to allow contracting up to 15 years. 
Approve the new Large Customer Owned Resiliency Program as described in the 2025 IRP.
Approve an additional sum of $4/kW-year AC for new demand response and new DER programs, including the Large Customer Owned Resiliency Program, Solar + Storage Pilot Program, and modified Customer Connected Solar Program.
Approve the V2G Pilot.
Customer Renewable Programs:
Approve the enhanced CARES subscription program, including the ability for participating customers to subscribe to smaller, DG resources; the opportunity for residential customers to subscribe through the DG Community Solar Program; more flexible participation provisions; and the ability for customers to identify renewable resources to be considered for procurement, as described in the 2025 IRP.
Approve modifications to the Customer Connected Solar Program as described in the 2025 IRP.
Approve the small commercial and residential Customer-Sited Solar Plus Storage Pilot Program as described in the 2025 IRP.
